15        c. It must be emphasized that these tables are based on a standard computer model

16 (Janus) used to simulate force-on-force engagements for training purposes. This model assesses

17 the effects of conventional and NBC weapons as a function of time. Computer modeling is not

18 perfect, and has limitations. However, the estimates shown above should give the planner an

19 adequate baseline to begin a detailed analysis and to develop a good medical support plan.

20

                                   2-14


---

                                                              FM 8-283

1      Section II. NUCLEAR INCIDENTS AND LOW-LEVEL RADIATION

2

3

4 2-8. General.

5

6      a. From 1944 to 1997, radiation incidents throughout the world involved mainly

7 radiation devices and isotopes. Accidents involving sealed sources of radiation accounted for

8 about 50 percent of these incidents. Also, a few very serious incidents involving nuclear

9 weapons have occurred throughout world. In addition to accidents, US forces have been recently

10 deployed to areas where there is an increased risk of exposure to radiological, chemical, and

11 biological contamination (Somalia and Eastern Europe, for example). US forces may be

12 operating in a theater that has nuclear reactor(s) with varying degrees of safety and containment.

13 Tactical dictates may require units to situate downwind of these reactors. The nuclear reactors

14 and their containment facilities may be lucrative enemy artillery or bombing targets because of

15 both the disruption of the electric power and the release of large quantities of radionuclides. The

16 resources then diverted to crisis and consequence management of irradiated US personnel and

17 host nation personnel may be overwhelming. Environmental radiological problems are of

18 special concern since at very low levels of radiation there will not be any immediate outward

19 signs of exposure. Other potential threats are those of industrial origins and radiological sources

20 that may be used as booby traps or dispersed to enhance exposure (RDD). This section will

21 discuss the most prevalent radiation sources that US forces could encounter because of an

22 incident and/or an emission of low-level radiation. For a detailed discussion of these potential

23 hazards, see Technical Guide (TG) 238, Radiological Sources of Potential Exposure and/or

24 Contamination.

25

26     b. Crucial issues for a response force during a nuclear incident are health and safety

27 concerns stemming from possible radioactive contamination, public affairs steps to address

28 public concerns, security of classified information, and recovery of the weapon or containment

29 of the radioactive source. Medical and environmental responders must identify the type of

30 radiation emitted because of the accident, estimate dose-equivalent limits, begin remedial

31 actions, initiate treatment, and institute countermeasures. In addition, planning must provide for

32 medical assistance, security, logistics, legal advice, site restoration, communication, and

33 response force integration. Intervention planning should also include countermeasures to limit

34 acute radiation syndrome and other nonstochastic effects as well as efforts to reduce the

35 collective dose so as to limit stochastic effects such as cancer induction and in utero exposures.

36

37

38 2-9. Natural Radioactive Sources

39

40     a. Background Radiation. Natural background radiation comes from the sun

41 (cosmic radiation), radioactive elements in the earth’s crust, rocks, air, water, and radioactive

42 materials in plants and the human body. Background radiation is unavoidable and the amount

43 varies from one location to another. Examples of background radiation are included in Table 2-

44 6.

45

46

                                     2-15


---

    FM 8-283

1                     Table 2-6. Examples of Background Radiation

2

3

    Source                     Radiation Type or Radionuclide

    Cosmic Radiation           Gamma, Electrons, Protons, Neutrons, and

    Earth’s Crust              Cosmic Rays
                               U, Th, 40K, Natural Decay Elements, and

                               Others

    Air                        Radon (222Rn), Thoron (220Rn), 3H, 14C, and

    Water                      Others
    Plants and the Human Body  U, Th, Ra, 40K and Others
                               226Ra, 228Ra, 3H, 14C, 40K, and Others

4

5

6           b. Cosmic Rays. Prior to entering the earth’s atmosphere, cosmic rays mainly

7 consist of protons and alpha particles. Upon interaction of cosmic rays with atoms in the
8 atmosphere, electrons, photons, protons, and neutrons are created. Carbon-14 (14C) and tritium
9 (3H) are also created by interactions of cosmic rays with the atmosphere. Ninety percent of the

10 tritium from cosmic rays is found in the ocean and other terrestrial waters. The remaining 10

11 percent are found in the stratosphere, where the tritium is directly produced by the cosmic ray

12 reactions.

13

14          c. Earth’s Crust. Soil contains radioactive materials such as uranium, radium,

15 thorium, potassium 40, and other elements. Some radioactive elements found in soil decay to
16 become part of the natural radiation decay series. For example, radon (222Rn) and thoron (220Rn)

17 are part of the decay process in the uranium and thorium series. Uranium is present in virtually

18 all rocks and soils, as well as in rivers and seawater. It is also associated with three of the four

19 naturally occurring radiation decay series. Uranium’s overall concentration in the earth’s crust is

20 around two parts per million (ppm). This equates roughly to 1 pound of uranium in every

21 500,000 pounds of earth. In its natural state, uranium is a slightly radioactive heavy metal and is

22 in an oxide form. Pure refined uranium metal is one of the most dense metals; it is about 65

23 percent more dense than lead. Hence, one of its military uses is as the depleted uranium (DU)

24 solid rod penetrator for the US 120-mm hypervelocity discarding sabot kinetic energy antitank

25 round (see paragraph 2-14). Radium 226 is also found in the soil, and naturally decays to Radon

26 222. Radon 222 is a radioactive gas that diffuses out of the ground and into the atmosphere, and

27 therefore, provides a large contribution to environmental radioactivity.

28

29          d. Air. Radon and thoron are radioactive gases that become airborne as they leak out

30 from the soil or building materials. Therefore, the amount of these gases present in the

31 atmosphere depends on the amount of the radioactive material present in the soil, the porosity of

32 the surface materials, ventilation, and meteorological conditions. Buildings without proper

33 ventilation can trap radon inside where it may accumulate to a point where it becomes a health

34 hazard. When one breathes air containing a large amount of radon, the radiation from its

35 progeny due to natural decay can damage the lungs. As mentioned before, Carbon-14 and

36 tritium are also present in the air because of interaction with cosmic rays.

37

                               2-16


---

                                                           FM 8-283

1   e. Water. The radioactive materials found in water depend upon the source of the

2 water. For example, well water may contain traces of uranium, thorium, radium, and it is often
3 high in radon; whereas, seawater may have higher concentrations of 40K. The tritium created in

4 the atmosphere by interaction with cosmic rays gradually descends into the lower part of the

5 atmosphere by natural diffusion. It is then brought down as tritiated water by rain or snow to the

6 earth’s surface, and accumulates in terrestrial waters. The testing of thermonuclear weapons in

7 the atmosphere brought an increase in the amount of tritium on the earth; however, tritium is

8 fairly uniformly distributed wherever water is present.

9

10  f. The Human Body and Plants. The human body and plants have radioactive

11 materials that have been transferred by the food chain. For example, bones contain radioactive

12 radium and other “bone seeker” radioisotopes. A typical human body contains about 100-125

13 micrograms of uranium. The daily uranium intake (largely from food ingestion) and excretion is

14 about 1.9 micrograms. Bone contains a few thousand picograms (pg) of uranium per gram of

15 bone ash; however, soft tissue concentrations are relatively low, in the order of a few hundred pg

16 per gram of wet tissue.

17

18  g. Average Yearly Dose. Every person on earth is exposed to ionizing radiation

19 every day. The average US resident receives a dose of about 360 millirem (mrem) per year. The

20 most significant contributor to this dose is radon, a gas created by the radioactive decay of

21 uranium. Since uranium occurs naturally in the ground and rocks, including building materials,

22 radon seeps into buildings where it concentrates in enclosed spaces and is inhaled. The exposure

23 rates in Figure 2-3 are for the average person. Note that the average person receives less than

24 one mrem per year from nuclear power production and fallout from atmospheric weapons

25 testing. The annual radiation dose that each person receives also varies by geographic location.

26 For example, the background radiation levels in Denver, Colorado are higher than those at sea

27 level, since it has less air between it and the sun than does a city at sea level. Air, although not

28 dense, still acts as a shield from cosmic rays.

29

30
31

32

33                          Figure 2-3. Yearly dose.

34

                                                    2-17


---

    FM 8-283

1 2-10. Sources from Nuclear Reactors and the Nuclear Fuel Cycle

2

3          a. Nuclear Reactors. As of 1999, there were 494 nuclear power plants worldwide;

4 444 were in operation in 1997, decreasing to 433 in operation in 1999. It is important to

5 understand the basic types, components, and waste products of reactors to understand the types

6 of radioactive exposure which might occur due to a nuclear reactor accident. Shown in Table 2-

7 7 is the worldwide distribution of nuclear plants by reactor type.

8

9

10                              Table 2-7. Types of Nuclear Reactors

11

    Reactor Type                         Percentage

    Pressurized Water Reactor (PWR)      58

    Boiling Water Reactor (BWR)          20

    Gas Cooled Reactor (GCR)             7

    Heavy Water Reactor (HWR)            11

    Light Water Graphite Reactor (LWGR)  3

    Fast Breeder Reactor (FBR)           1

12

13

14 Typical components of nuclear power reactors are discussed below:

15

16                  (1) Reactor pressure vessel. In most reactors, the fuel is contained within a

17 single pressure vessel. However, in some designs, the fuel rods are contained within individual

18 pressure tubes.

19

20                  (2) Fuel. The fuel is usually an arrangement of pellets of enriched uranium

21 oxide (UO2) in zircalloy rods, which are placed in the reactor core. Most commercial reactors
22 use UO2 as fuel, although some breeder reactor and gas cooled reactor designs may use
23 plutonium oxide (PO2) and thorium oxide (ThO2) as fuel.

24

25                  (3) Moderator. The moderator is used to slow down the fast moving neutrons

26 produced by fission. This allows them to be captured by other nuclei so that the fission process

27 continues. The moderator may be in the form of a solid, such as graphite, or a liquid, such as

28 water.

29

30                  (4) Control rods. Control rods are made of neutron absorbing materials such

31 as cadmium or boron and are strengthened with steel. They are moved into or out of the reactor

32 core to control the rate of fission or to stop the reaction completely.

33

34                  (5) Coolant. The coolant is a liquid or a gas circulating through the reactor,

35 transferring heat away from the core generally in the form of steam; see (6) below.

36

37                  (6) Steam generator. The steam generator is part of the cooling system of a

38 PWR. It separates steam from the coolant and directs it to the turbines to generate electricity.

                                         2-18


---

                                                                                              FM 8-283

1            (7) Containment. Reactors are contained inside a casing that acts as a

2 radiation shield and is designed to prevent the release of radioactivity into the environment.

3 Lack of adequate containment is a design weakness of some former Soviet Union designed

4 reactors.

5

6            (8) Other Components. Other important components include the reactor

7 pumps, turbines, pressurizer (in PWRs), and demineralizers.

8

9   b. Waste from Nuclear Reactors. The PWR is the most common type of nuclear

10 power plant in the world. Therefore, its waste has become the most common type of radioactive

11 waste from nuclear power plants. This waste is generated from liquid, solid, and gaseous

12 effluents. Most facilities classify their waste as clean, dirty, and laundry wastes. The terms

13 clean and dirty refer to the chemical purity of the water and not to the amounts of radioactivity.

14 For example, the primary system waste that originates in the primary coolant circuit is essentially

15 chemically pure water, but has the highest level of radioactivity.

16

17           (1) Liquid waste. The presence of radioactive materials in the reactor coolant

18 system (RCS) is a result of neutron activation of chemicals, impurities, and the water contained

19 in the system, and from leakage due to defects in the reactor fuel rods. The radioactive material

20 is transferred from the fuel to other parts of the plant via the RCS, coolant cleanup systems, heat

21 exchangers, and so forth. A holdup tank is used to collect the liquid for about 30 days in order to

22 allow the decay of radionuclides with a short half-life. Radioactive liquid waste is then

23 decontaminated by evaporation or demineralization.

24

25           (a) In the evaporation process, water is boiled off and the steam is

26 condensed. The evaporator residue (solids) contains most of the radioactive material and can be

27 disposed in a controlled manner. The condensed water is essentially, although not completely,

28 free of dissolved solids, but will contain all of the tritium. The tritiated water is vaporized and

29 then condensed with the ordinary water in the evaporator.

30

31           (b) In the demineralization process, an ion exchange resin is used

32 which is similar to the material used in household and industrial water softeners. Most of the

33 dissolved matter is removed and retained by the resin when the radioactive wastewater is passed

34 through the demineralizer. Elements such as cesium, yttrium, and molybdenum are removed

35 relatively slowly by demineralizers, and tritium is essentially unaffected.

36

37           (2) Gaseous waste. Gaseous effluents from power plants contain noble gases

38 such as krypton and xenon, iodine, and particulate matter. Particulate matter is reduced by

39 filtering the gas through a high efficiency particle arrestor (HEPA) filter prior to discharge. A

40 charcoal filter removes part of the iodine. The noble gases are difficult to remove, hence they

41 are held for a time to permit radioactive decay. The gasses are compressed and are held for
42 about 60 days. Krypton-85 (85Kr), 133Xe, 133I, and tritium are the radionuclides that remain in

43 appreciable amount. These gasses are filtered and mixed with large volumes of ventilation air

44 prior to discharge through the stack.

45

                                          2-19


---

    FM 8-283

1               (3) Solid Waste. Solid waste actually includes the spent fuel and reactor

2 components themselves. Aside from these items, solid waste generally includes the spent ion

3 exchange (demineralizer) resins, discarded filter material (including charcoal), and evaporator

4 residues. The residues are often slurries rather than solids, but are usually solidified by mixing

5 with cement.

6

7   c. Radiation Exposure Pathways. Table 2-8 shows the principal radiation exposure

8 pathways from nuclear power plant effluents. Because of their relatively large yield in uranium

9 fission and known affinity to be deposited in the thyroid (a critical organ), iodine-131 and iodine-

10 133 are very important sources of exposure. Cesium-134 and Cesium-137 are the radioisotopes

11 produced in significant amounts during fission, and, if ingested, exposes all organs to beta-

12 gamma radiation. Cesium can enter the body by drinking water or milk, or by eating fish from

13 the general vicinity of the liquid effluent discharged from the plant. The internal (lung) dose

14 from inhalation of radioactive isotopes of noble gases is very small. If adequate holdup is
15 provided before release into the atmosphere, exposure to short-lived noble gases, such as 133Xe
16 and 88Kr can be minimized. However, there may be significant exposure to long-lived 85Kr if it

17 is allowed to accumulate in the atmosphere. Tritium may also present an exposure risk if

18 allowed to accumulate in the liquid and gaseous effluents and in the surrounding environment.

19

2

3   d. Waste from Nuclear Fuel Cycle.

4

5                 (1) The nuclear fuel cycle includes all the activities associated with the

6 production of electricity from nuclear reactions. This includes mining of the fuel, milling,

7 conversion, enrichment, fuel fabrication, the reaction, and the resultant spent fuel. Waste from

8 the nuclear fuel cycle is categorized in a number of ways, including high, medium, or low-level

9 waste. These wastes come from virtually all phases of the cycle and include--

10

11                         •     Essentially nonradioactive waste resulting from mining.

12

13                         •     Low-level waste produced at all stages of the fuel cycle.

14

15                         •     Intermediate-level waste produced during reactor operation and by

16 reprocessing.

17

18                         •     High-level waste, which is spent fuel and waste containing fission

19 products from reprocessing.

20

21                (2) Milling waste contains long-lived radioactive materials and progeny in

22 low concentrations and toxic materials such as heavy metals; however, the total mass activity of

23 radioactive elements in milling waste is less than the original ore. This waste requires safe

24 management in order to isolate it from the environment or to ensure that releases are limited to

25 reduce environmental impact. Normally, shallow burial in engineered facilities is used to

26 dispose of milling waste.

27

28                (3) The chemical conversion process of turning uranium hexafluoride into

29 dioxide produces liquid waste that contains chemical impurities, including fluorides. Lime is

30 used to treat the effluent in order to precipitate calcium fluoride that contains traces of uranium

31 and some decay products.

32

33                (4) The fuel enrichment process leads to the production of depleted uranium

34 (DU). This is uranium in which the concentration of 235U (radioactive isotope) is significantly

35 less than the 0.7 percent found in nature. This material, which is primarily 238U (non-radioactive

36 isotope), is used in applications where a high density material is required. Some of the

37 applications include radiation shielding, production of mixed oxide (MOX) fuels, main battle
38 tank armor, and kinetic energy armor piercing rounds (see paragraph 2-14). While 238U is not

39 fissionable, it is a low specific activity radioactive material and some precautions must be taken

40 in its storage and disposal.

41

42

43

                                 2-21


---

    FM 8-283

1 2-11. Biomedical Sources

2

3   a. Sources of Biomedical Radiation. Biomedical sources of radiation are those

4 devices or materials that are readily available at hospitals and some laboratories, which could

5 present a hazard if individuals are exposed or if the materials are dispersed into the surrounding

6 environment. An example would be the intentional release of radionuclides into the environment

7 from a nuclear medicine department due to a terrorist-caused explosion and fire. Biomedical

8 uses of radiation are roughly broken down into two areas--diagnosis and therapy. Diagnosis

9 includes routine x-rays, fluoroscopic examinations, computed tomography exams, and exams

10 performed in nuclear medicine such as injections of radioactive materials. Therapy is primarily

11 treatment for cancer, and includes radiation-producing equipment such as linear accelerators,

12 radioisotope-generating machines, and radiation from brachytherapy sources.

13

14  b. Diagnostic Radiation Sources. For all of the devices discussed below, accidents

15 can be precluded by simply having the devices turned off. However, during operation, the three

16 basic ways to reduce external doses can be applied (minimize time and maximize distance and

17 shielding). The following are common radiation-producing diagnostic devices and materials:

18

19            (1) X-ray units. X-ray radiation is produced by accelerating electrons through

20 an electrical voltage potential. As soon as the switch is released, or the pre-set exposure time is

21 reached, x-ray production ends. Many x-ray-type devices that use a high voltage and a source of

22 electrons produce x-rays as an unwanted byproduct of device operation. These are called

23 incidental x-rays.

24

25            (2) Computed tomography. Computed tomography (CT) is a diagnostic

26 procedure in which a cross-sectional picture of a slice of the body is made. Sometimes a contrast

27 material such as iodine is injected into the patient’s vein. Modern CT scanners use narrow x-ray

28 beams that concentrate directly on the area or organs under study. Computed tomography

29 scanners do not present a hazard unless they are turned on and nearby personnel do not take

30 routine shielding and distance precautions.

31

32            (3) Fluoroscopy. Fluoroscopy combines an x-ray unit and an image

33 intensifier that allows a real time view on a screen of the patient and any procedures that are

34 being performed on the patient. An x-ray beam passes through the patient through a series of

35 intensifier tubes, fluorescent screens, and photocathodes. This produces light photons, that in

36 turn, produce an image on an output screen. As with other x-ray devices, fluoroscopy units are

37 not a danger unless they are in operation and personnel are within the beam field.

38

39            (4) Diagnostic nuclear medicine. This branch of medicine uses a radiation

40 source positioned in the body to image a person’s anatomy and to provide information about the

41 functioning of a specific organ(s). Radiopharmaceutical tracers are attracted to specific organs,

42 bones, or tissues. By putting a radioactive isotope in the body, its path through the body can be

43 tracked by an external monitor. A special type of camera is used, a gamma or positron emission

44 tomography (PET), or a single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) camera. These

45 cameras transform the emissions into images and data on the particular organ or process being

46 imaged.

                                                2-22


---

                                                                   FM 8-283

1   c. Therapeutic Radiation Sources. Radiation therapy departments treat patients for

2 cancer or certain benign conditions. The most common types of therapy equipment are cobalt

3 teletherapy equipment and linear accelerators. Rapidly dividing cells are particularly sensitive to

4 damage by radiation. Therefore, some cancerous growths can be controlled or eliminated by

5 irradiating the area containing the growth. Some cancers are treated with sealed radioactive

6 sources placed directly in the tissue, the body cavity, or on the body surfaces. This type of

7 treatment is called brachytherapy.

8

9                  (1) The cobalt-60 unit. Of all potential radionuclides, 60Co has proved to be

10 the most suitable and most commonly used for external beam radiotherapy. The 60Co source is

11 usually in the form of a solid cylinder, disc, or pallet and is contained inside a stainless steel
12 capsule and sealed by welding. The 60Co decays to 60Ni with the emission of beta particles and

13 two photons of energy; these gamma rays constitute the useful treatment beam. Caution must be

14 taken when handling teletherapy units because sources in the units may not be in the shielded

15 position and therefore may be a serious radiological hazard.

16

17                 (2) The gamma knife. The gamma knife delivers a single, high dose of

18 ionizing radiation by sending beams from 201 separate 60Co sources through a device known as

19 the collimator helmet. Only at the point where all 201 beams converge at a single, finely focused

20 point is enough radiation delivered to treat the diseased tissue. However, nearby tissue is spared.

21 The gamma knife can present a radiological hazard if the shielding mechanisms fail, or if the
22 60Co source unit is involved in an explosion or fire.

23

24                 (3) Heavy particle beam generators. Heavy particle beam generators,

25 cyclotrons, and linear accelerators produce heavy particle beams which include neutrons,

26 protons, deuterons, alpha particles, and so forth, accelerated to high energies. Their use in

27 radiotherapy is still experimental; however, they are undergoing clinical trials, and there is a

28 certain amount of risk involved with this type of equipment.

29

30                 (4) Brachytherapy. Brachytherapy is a method of treatment in which sealed

31 radioactive sources are used to deliver radiation at a short distance by interstitial (implants),

32 intracavity, or surface application. A high radiation dose can be delivered locally to the tumor

33 with rapid dose fall off in the surrounding tissue. The radioactive sources are fabricated in the

34 form of needles, wires, or seeds, which can be inserted into the tissue. These sources need to be

35 handled in accordance with the proper safety precautions to preclude any unnecessary dangers.

36 Table 2-9 shows some of the common radionuclides used in brachytherapy and their half-life.

37

38

39                 Table 2-9. Radionuclides used in Brachytherap.

40

    Radionuclide                      Half-Life

    Cobalt-60                         5.26 Years

    Palladium-103                     17.0 Days

    Iodine-125                        60.2 Days

    Cesium-137                        30.0 Years

    Iridium-192                       74.2 Days

                                      2-23


---

    FM 8-283

    Gold-198                                    2.7 Days

    Radon-222                                   3.83 Days

    Radium-226                                  1600 Years

3 2-12. Industrial Sources

4

5 United States personnel can also be exposed to radiation from industrial processes involved with

6 agricultural, scientific research, manufacturing, and educational facilities. Exposure from these

7 sources may not be common, but accidental exposures can result in serious consequences. The

8 use of radiation and radioactive materials can be found in many sectors of industry and science.

9 Under normal operating conditions, most industrial sources of radiation present minimal

10 exposure risks when used safely. However, US personnel must always be aware of their

11 surroundings and be alert to the possibility of danger from these sources. Table 2-10 summarizes

12 some of the industrial sources of radiation. Also, some of the more common sources are

13 discussed in detail.
