3

4 A-3. Biological Dosimetry

5

 6 A crude estimate of absorbed dose can be obtained from the clinical presentation and assessment
 7 of the response of actively proliferating cell systems to radiation. Uncertainties in dose estimates
 8 arise largely from the high variability between individuals and other factors such as infection.
 9 Generally, the sensitivity of these bio-indicators is poor; and, given the transient nature of the
10 signs and symptoms in sub-lethal doses, their clinical usefulness is limited. A biological
11 dosimetry is recommended in cases where medical treatment decisions are warranted.
12 Knowledge of the distribution heterogeneity of the absorbed dose is particularly important with
13 respect to medical treatment decisions for patients exhibiting radiation-induced bone marrow
14 syndrome. Reliable biodosimetry is also necessary for validation of suspected low-level
15 radiation exposures.

16

17            a. Analysis of Cytological Assays - Chromosome Aberrations. The analysis of

18 chromosomal aberrations in peripheral blood lymphocytes is a biological dosimetry method that

19 is widely used to assess radiation dose. Even in partial body exposures, chromosome damage is

20 an excellent indicator of the absorbed dose. Many types of chromosomal aberrations may appear

21 in lymphocytes following exposure to radiation, but dicentric (a chromosome with two

22 centromeres) is a well-accepted biomarker for ionizing radiation exposure and is used to estimate

23 radiation dose. The incidence of dicentrics in blood lymphocytes for the general population is 1

24 in 1,000 to 2,000 metaphases. Human T-lymphocytes have a long half-life; and a small

25 proportion of them survives for decades. The frequency of dicentrics following exposure

26 remains fairly stable up to a few weeks. After acute partial exposure, the irradiated lymphocytes

27 rapidly mix with unirradiated blood, and equilibrium is reached within 24 hours.

28

29                 (1) Dicentric assay--technical aspects

30

31                 (a) Sample collection. Ten to 15 ml of blood from the exposed subject

32 is collected in a heparinized blood collection tube. The blood lymphocytes are isolated in the

33 cytogenetic laboratory and are stimulated to grow in culture. Cell proliferation is arrested in the

                             A-6


---

                                                                             FM 8-283

1 first metaphase following stimulation; and metaphase spreads are prepared. The stained first-

2 division metaphase spreads are observed under a microscope for enumerating dicentrics.

3

4                       (b) Dose-response calibration curves and factors influencing dose

5 estimation. In vitro and in vivo irradiation of blood lymphocytes produces similar yields of

6 dicentrics per Gy. Therefore, observed levels of dicentrics in an exposed individual can be

7 related to dose by use of an in vitro established dose-response calibration curve. In vitro

8 calibration curves have been generated for a variety of radiation qualities, including all those

9 likely to be encountered in an accident scenario (see Figure A-1). Within any given laboratory,

10 the calibration curves have proved very reproducible for lymphocytes examined in their first-

11 division metaphase.

12

13

14  Figure A-1, Armed Forces Radiobiology Research Institute dose-response

15                      curves for dicentric induction in human lymhocytes.

16

17

18                      (c) Metaphases. Two hundred to 500 metaphases are sufficient to

19 estimate a dose at irradiation levels of medical significance. The statistical uncertainty of the

20 dicentric yield is the main component of the 95 percent confidence limit on the dose estimate

21 with a few hundred cells scored from an irradiated subject. The influence of the number of

22 metaphases analyzed and the number of dicentrics scored on the 95 percent confidence limit on

23 the dose estimate is presented in Table A-3.

24

25

26

27

                                                 A-7

2

    Number of    Number of cells          Lower             Upper      Mean dose**
    dicentrics       analyzed      confidence limit  confidence limit      (mGy)

                                          (mGy)             (mGy)

              0               200  0                 470                                   -

              1               200  30                610                     200

5

6

7                (2) Automated analysis. Some of the shortcomings associated with

8 metaphase-dicentric chromosome-aberration analysis are being overcome by automation.

9 Automated metaphase finding on slides, using image analysis techniques, significantly enhances

10 the speed of this assay. Accurate identification of dicentrics on metaphase spreads is possible by

11 centromere painting, either by immunoenzymatic methodology or fluorescence in situ

12 hybridization (FISH). This helps in automated enumeration of dicentrics for rapid and reliable

13 dose estimates; and, with increases in the number of cells analyzed, confidence limits on dose

14 estimates are improved.

15

16               (3) Translocation assay. In cases of retrospective dosimetry, another

17 subclass of chromosome aberrations, reciprocal translocations have been used for dose

18 assessment instead of scoring dicentric chromosome aberrations. Reciprocal translocations are

19 persistent and are formed in similar yields as dicentrics. They are typically detected using

20 specific whole chromosome DNA hybridization probes and FISH methodology.

21

22               (4) Premature-chromosome condensation assay. In contrast to the

23 enumeration of dicentrics in stimulated lymphocytes, no stimulation of cell proliferation is

24 required for the premature-chromosome condensation assay. Polyethylene-mediated fusion of

25 blood lymphocytes with mitotic cells results in a premature condensation of lymphocyte-

26 chromatin material into discrete chromatid-like structures. Blood lymphocytes from normal

27 subjects display a distribution around a mean of 46 prematurely condensed chromosomes

28 (PCCs). Increases in the number of PCCs are evident after exposure to radiation and 24 hours of

29 repair. Based on the increase in the number of PCCs, dose estimates can be obtained within 48

30 hours of receipt of blood in the laboratory. Radiation induced mitotic delay does not interfere

31 with the assay since it is performed on interphase nuclei and does not require cell division. The

32 method is envisioned to be applicable after partial body and/or supra-lethal exposure.

                                   A-8


---

                                                                                 FM 8-283

1

2   (5) Micronucleus assay. A micronucleus is a small chromatin body seen

3 outside the nucleus of a daughter cell following cell division. A micronucleus is formed as a

4 result of unincorporated chromatin material that largely arises from acentric fragments. The

5 frequency of micronuclei in an exposed individual's blood lymphocytes is dose dependent, and

6 therefore, can be used as a biodosimeter. However, micronuclei are not specific to radiation

7 exposure. Discrimination between total and partial-body exposure is more difficult. High doses

8 of radiation interfere with cell division. High base-line frequency and age dependency make the

9 reliability of this assay questionable.

10

11  (6) Biochemical endpoints-- deoxyribonucleic acid single strand breaks and

12 base alterations. Deoxyribonucleic acid single-strand breaks (SSB) and base alterations are

13 formed following exposure to ionizing radiation. The dose-response relationship for SSB and

14 altered-base yields and the effect of repair at various times post-exposure are well known for

15 various nucleated blood-cell components and can be used for dose assessment.

16

17  b. Biophysical Assays--Electron-Spin Resonance. Persistent free radicals formed in

18 solid matrix biomaterials (dental enamel, nail clippings, and hair) from exposed personnel can be

19 detected via electron-spin resonance. These measurements can provide reliable biophysical dose

20 estimates as well as partial-body exposure information. In some circumstances, certain materials

21 from clothing, particularly hard plastics and buttons may be measured and an estimate of

22 absorbed dose made.

                          Section II. DEFINITIONS

acute radiation syndrome  a combination of clinical syndromes occurring in stages, during a
                          period of hours to weeks after exposure, as radiation injury to
                          various tissues and organs is expressed.

afterwind                 wind currents that are created near a nuclear explosion by the
                          updraft accompanying the rise of the fireball and that travel toward
                          the center of the blast.

agranulocytosis           a complex of symptoms characterized by marked decrease in the
                          number of granulocytes and by lesions of the throat and other
                          mucous membranes, of the gastrointestinal tract, and of the skin.

airburst                  the explosion of a nuclear weapon at such a height that the
                          expanding fireball does not contact the earth's surface.

alpha particle            a positively charged particle ejected from the nucleus of a
                          radioactive atom, being a high-speed ionized atom of helium. A
                          stream of these particles constitutes alpha rays.

aplasia                   lack of development of an organ or tissue, or of the cellular
                          products from an organ or tissue.

atomic number             the number of protons in an atomic nucleus.

beta particle             an electron emitted from an atomic nucleus during beta decay.

bioassay sampling         indirect means of measuring contamination in body tissue or fluids
                          from which body burden is extrapolated.

blast wave                a pulse of air in which the pressure increases sharply at the front
                          propagated by the explosion.

bremsstrahlung            the process by which a beta particle emits an X-ray photon during
                          its interaction with an atomic nucleus.

carcinogenesis            the development of cancer; various malignant growths that
                          manifest invasiveness and a tendency to metastasize to another
                          location.

cataractogenesis          the development of cataracts; opacity of the lens causing blindness.

                          Glossary-7


---

FM 8-283              absence of an oxygen supply to the brain despite adequate
cerebral anoxia       perfusion of the tissue by blood.
congestion
critical mass         excessive or abnormal accumulation of blood in a tissue or organ.
critical organ
cytokine              the minimum amount of a fissionable material needed to sustain a
                      nuclear chain reaction for a given set of circumstances.
decontamination
delayed fallout       body organ with an affinity for a particular substance and in which
                      that substance concentrates.
desquamation
dose rate             a nonantibody protein released by one cell population that acts as
                      an intercellular mediator on another cell population(s). Synthetic
deterministic effect  cytokines are metabolically active in pico-molar concentrations.
dynamic pressure
early fallout         reduction or removal of radioactive contamination from a
edema                 structure, area, object, or person.
exposure
                      radioactive fallout that returns to earth later than 24 hours after a
                      nuclear detonation; usually ascends into the stratosphere and is
                      distributed worldwide.

                      the shedding of epithelial elements, chiefly of the skin, in scales or
                      small sheets; exfoliation.

                      a measure of the amount of ionizing radiation an individual is
                      exposed to, per unit of time; commonly expressed in units of gray
                      (or rads) per hour.

                      one that occurs after a certain dose threshold, with the severity of
                      the effect determined by the dose; example: cataractogenesis.

                      air pressure that results from the wind behind the shock front of a
                      blast wave.

                      radioactive fallout that returns to earth within 24 hours after a
                      nuclear detonation; also referred to as local fallout.

                      the presence of abnormally large amounts of fluid in the
                      intercellular tissue.

                      a measure of the number of ionizations produced by gamma or X-
                      rays in a volume of air; expressed in units of roentgen.

fallout                                                                                 FM 8-283

free-in-air-dose   hot gases that form a luminous sphere after a nuclear explosion.

hematopoietic

hyperpyrexia

ingestion pathway  electromagnetic radiation of short wavelengths emitted by the
                   nucleus of an atom during a nuclear reaction. They consist of
                   high-energy photons, have no mass and no electric charge, travel
                   with the speed of light, and are usually associated with beta rays.

                   any cell containing granules in its cytoplasm, especially a
                   leukocyte (neutrophil, basophil, or eosinophil).

                   agranulocytosis; a symptom complex consisting of a marked
                   decrease in the number of circulating white blood cells, with
                   lesions of the throat and mucous membranes.

                   pertaining to, or effecting, the formation of blood cells.

                   the escape of blood from the vessels; hemorrhages, classified

                   1 centimeter) in diamerter).

                   a highly elevated body temperature.

                   abnormally low blood pressure.

                   radioactivity produced in certain materials as a result of the capture
                   of neutrons, which is accompanied by the formation of unstable
                   nuclei.

                   route for internalization of radioactive contaminants; the pathway
                   most accessible for decontamination.

inhalation pathway
ionization            the process of stripping electrons from their atomic orbits by

malformation

nadir

agent
                      a birth defect; an abnormal structure or form; example: small head.

                      the point at which a blood count drops to, or closest to zero, before

                      an electrically neutral or uncharged particle of matter existing

                      traditionally, uranium or plutonium used to produce a nuclear

                                     Glossary-10


---

                                                                                      FM 8-283

nuclide             an atomic nucleus with a specific energy state and specific number
                    of protons and neutrons.

orbital excitation  change in energy level of an orbital electron that occurs when the
                    energy lost by the incident radiation is insufficient to cause
                    ionization.

overpressure        the transient pressure that is created by the shock wave of an
                    explosion and exceeds the ambient pressure; expressed in pounds
                    per square inch.

pathognomonic       specifically distinctive or characteristic of a disease or pathologic
                    condition; a sign or symptom on which a diagnosis can be based.

perceived threat    a threat that is experienced by a person subjectively and out of
                    proportion to the real threat or physical danger.

phagocytosis        the engulfing of microorganisms, other cells, and foreign particles
                    by phagocytes.

pressor             tending to increase blood pressure.

prodrome            a premonitory symptom or precursor; a symptom indicating the
                    onset of a disease.

Prussian blue       ferric ferrocyanide; a chemical that is not absorbed by the
                    gastrointestinal system and is an effective means to reduce the
                    body's uptake of cesium, thallium, and rubidium; approved as an
                    investigational new drug by the United States Food and Drug
                    Administration with the license held by Radiation Emergency
                    Assistance Center/Training Site.

radioactive cloud   an all-inclusive term for the cloud of hot gases, smoke, dust, dirt,
                    and debris from a weapon and the environment. The cloud is
                    carried aloft in conjunction with the rising fireball produced by the
                    detonation of a nuclear weapon.

radioactive contamination radioactive material in an undesirable location such as on
                                    structures, areas, objects, or personnel.

radionuclide        a radioactive nuclide; one that disintegrates with the emission of
                    particulate or electromagnetic radiations.

rainout             the removal of radioactive particles from a nuclear cloud by
                    precipitation when the cloud is within a rain cloud.

                    Glossary-11

FM 8-283             the loss of the ability to reproduce. Many organs must have cells
reproductive death   that can reproduce to function. Thus, even though injured cells
                     may remain biologically viable, reproductive death may cause
scavenging           irreversible organ damage.
sepsis
septicemia           the selective removal of material from the radioactive cloud by
skin permeability    inert substances, such as precipitation, introduced into the fireball.

Specific ionization  the presence of pathogenic microorganisms (bacteria) or their
stochastic effect    toxins in the blood or other tissues.

stratosphere         systemic disease associated with the presence and persistence of
subsurface burst     pathogenic microorganisms or their toxins in the blood.
syndrome
synergistic          the rate at which the skin absorbs a liquid; expressed as a
thermal radiation    coefficient. The lower a substance's coefficient, the greater the rate
total parenteral     of absorption.
nutrition
tropopause           number of ion pairs per unit distance formed along the path of a
troposphere          particle, often expressed as ion pairs per centimeter.

                     an effect that has no-dose threshold and for which the severity of
                     the effect is not dose-related, although its probability is; example:
                     carcinogenesis

                     a relatively stable layer of the atmosphere extending from the
                     tropopause to an altitude of about 30 miles.

                     the explosion of a nuclear weapon beneath the surface of the earth.

                     a set of symptoms that occur together.

                     acting together to enhance the effect of another force or agent.

                     electromagnetic radiation (infrared, visible, and ultraviolet) emitted
                     from the fireball of a nuclear explosion as a consequence of high
                     temperatures.

                     by injection through some route other than the alimentary canal.

                     the layer of the earth's atmosphere that separates the troposphere
                     from the stratosphere.

                     the layer of the earth's atmosphere extending from the surface up to
                     the tropopause, normally about 25,000 feet in temperate zones and
                     about 55,000 feet in the topics.

                                  FM 8-283

washout              the removal of radioactive particles from a nuclear cloud by
weapon debris        precipitation when the nuclear cloud is below a rain or snow cloud.
wound contamination
                     highly radioactive material consisting of fission products, various
                     products of neutron capture, unspent fuel, and shards of bomb
                     casing that remain after a nuclear explosion.

                     the presence of a radioactive substance in a wound, whether an
                     abrasion, puncture, or laceration; condition in which the loss of
                     intact skin increases the risk that the contaminant will be absorbed.
